\displaystyle 0)

It is easy to verify that the sum of any two adjacent vectors in the above sequence is unique, which gives possible values for
e_{i,j}
’s. Unfortunately, numbers constructed in this way do not necessarily satisfy that
E_{i}\cap E_{i^{\prime}}=\emptyset
. In particular, there might exist some pair
i^{\prime},k^{\prime}
with
k^{\prime}=\tau(i^{\prime})
where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[j]={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i^{\prime}}[j]
for
j\leq\gamma-2
, and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{k}[j]={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{k^{\prime}}[j]
for
j\geq\gamma-1
. In this case, we have

({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i^{\prime}}[0],{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i^{\prime}}[1],\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{k^{\prime}}[{\gamma-1}],{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{k^{\prime}}[{\gamma}],0)=({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[0],{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[1],\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{k}[{\gamma-1}],{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{k}[{\gamma}],0),

violating
E_{i}\cap E_{i^{\prime}}=\emptyset
.

How can we construct unique
e_{i,j}
’s?
Towards this, we consider all the one-to-one mappings
g:{\cal{S}}^{\gamma+1}_{d}\rightarrow{\cal{S}}^{\gamma+1}_{d}
. Under composition of functions, all such mappings form a group
Aut({\cal{S}}^{\gamma+1}_{d})
. We are interested in the special mapping that maps each
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}
to
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{k}
(which corresponds to the permutation
\tau
), which belongs to
Aut({\cal{S}}^{\gamma+1}_{d})
. We show that any mapping in
Aut({\cal{S}}^{\gamma+1}_{d})
, and hence this special mapping, can be decomposed into a sequence of simple mappings. More precisely, we consider any finite set
{\cal{M}}
and the group
Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
of one-to-one mappings from
{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}
to itself. We call a mapping in
Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
an
h
-shuffler if this mapping only changes the
h
-th coordinate of the input vector, i.e., an
h
-shuffler
f
satisfies that for any
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}\in{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}
,

f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}})=f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[0],{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[1],\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[\upsilon-1])=({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[0],\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[h-1],z,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[h+1],\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[\upsilon-1])),

for some
z\in{\cal{M}}
.
We show the following group theoretic lemma which states that any mapping of
Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
can be decomposed into
2\upsilon

h
-shufflers; see Appendix
E
for the proof. Our
e_{i,j}
’s can be obtained from these
h
-shufflers.

Lemma 8
.

Let
{\cal{M}}
be a finite set,
\upsilon\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and
Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
be the group of all one-to-one mappings from
{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}
to itself. The group operation is function composition and denoted as
\circ
. For any
\pi\in Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
, there exist
h
-shufflers
f_{h},\hat{f}_{h}\in Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
for every
1\leq h\leq\upsilon
such that
F({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}})=\hat{F}(\pi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}))
for any
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}\in{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}
, where
F=f_{\upsilon-1}\circ f_{\upsilon-2}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}
and
\hat{F}=\hat{f}_{\upsilon-1}\circ\hat{f}_{\upsilon-2}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{f}_{0}
. Furthermore,
f_{h}
’s and
\hat{f}_{h}
’s can be constructed in time that is polynomial in
|{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}|
.

Lemma
8
implies a decomposition of
\pi
into
h
-shufflers, i.e.,
\pi=\hat{f}_{0}^{-1}\circ\hat{f}_{1}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{f}_{\upsilon-1}^{-1}\circ f_{\upsilon-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}
.

With Lemma
8
, we are ready to prove Lemma
7
. We first set the value of all parameters.
Towards this, we will apply Lemma
6
twice.

At first, we apply Lemma
6
by taking

N=n
. Then we obtain
\sigma:\mathbb{Z}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{n^{\prime}}
where
n^{\prime}=n^{1+{\mathcal{O}}(1/\sqrt{\log\log n})}
,
\sigma(i)=\sum_{j=0}^{\gamma}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[j]x^{j}
for
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[j]\in{\cal{S}}_{d}
where
\gamma=\lceil\frac{\log n}{\log\log n}\rceil+{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\log n}{(\log\log n)^{3/2}})
,
d=\lceil e^{(\sqrt{\log\log n}+7)^{2}}\rceil=e^{{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\log\log n})}\cdot\log n
and
x=5d+1
. Except
N
, all the other parameters, including
n^{\prime},\sigma,x,d,\gamma
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}
’s are fixed throughout the following part of this section.

Next, we apply Lemma
6
again by setting
N=4\gamma+4
where
\gamma
takes the value we determined above. By doing so we obtain another injection
\sigma^{\prime}
. We have the following simple observation.

Observation 1
.

If
\ell\leq 4\gamma+4
, then
\sigma^{\prime}(\ell)=o(\log^{2}n)<x^{2}
.

Proof.

Note that
\gamma=O(\frac{\log n}{\log\log n})
. By Lemma
6
,
\sigma^{\prime}(\ell)\leq(4\gamma+4)^{1+O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log\log(4\gamma+4)}})}=o(\gamma^{2})=o(\log^{2}n)
.
∎

Next, We will apply Lemma
8
. In the following part of this paper, any
\upsilon
-dimensional vector
\textstyle\bf c
represents the number given by the polynomial expression
\sum_{i=1}^{\upsilon-1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[i]x^{i}
; vectors and polynomial expressions are used interchangeably. Given our permutation
\tau
, we define
\hat{\tau}
as a one-to-one mapping that maps each vector
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}
to
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{k}
, or equivalently, maps
\sigma(i)
to
\sigma(k)
if
k=\tau(i)
. Notice that
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}
’s form a subset of
{\cal{S}}_{d}^{\gamma+1}
, so currently
\hat{\tau}
is only defined on this subset. We can extend
\hat{\tau}
to
{\cal{S}}_{d}^{\gamma+1}
such that for
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}\in{\cal{S}}_{d}^{\gamma+1}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}\neq{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}
, then
\hat{\tau}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}})={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}
. Hence,
\hat{\tau}\in Aut({\cal{S}}_{d}^{\gamma+1})
. According to Lemma
8
, we can obtain
h
-shufflers
f_{h}
and
\hat{f}_{h}
for all
0\leq h\leq\gamma
such that
f_{\gamma}\circ f_{\gamma-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}=\hat{f}_{\gamma}\circ\hat{f}_{\gamma-1}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{f}_{0}\circ\hat{\tau}
.

For ease of notation, define
F_{h}=f_{h}\circ f_{h-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}
and
\hat{F}_{h}=\hat{f}_{h}\circ\hat{f}_{h-1}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{f}_{0}
. As
h
-shufflers only changes the
h
-th coordinate, we have the following observation.

Observation 2
.

The following statements are true:

•

For any
0\leq h\leq\gamma

(F_{\gamma}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[h]=(F_{\gamma-1}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[h]=\cdots=(F_{h}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[h],

(\hat{F}_{\gamma}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[h]=(\hat{F}_{\gamma-1}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[h]=\cdots=(\hat{F}_{h}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[h];

•

For any
0\leq h\leq\gamma
,

(F_{h-1}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[h]=(F_{h-2}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[h]=\cdots=(F_{0}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[h]={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[h],

(\hat{F}_{h-1}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[h]=(\hat{F}_{h-2}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[h]=\cdots=(\hat{F}_{0}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[h]={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[h].

Now we are ready to construct a unique linking sequence for every
i
. Intuitively, note that since
\sigma^{\prime}(h)<x^{2}
, then
\sigma^{\prime}(h)
occupies two “bits”in the polynomial (that is, two coordinates). With this in mind, we let
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(i)=(0,\sigma^{\prime}(i))
. Moreover, we define
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(0)=(0,0)
. Now consider the following two sequences, starting from
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}
and

\displaystyle(

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[0],

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[1],\hskip 9.24994pt\cdots,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[{\gamma-1}],

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[{\gamma}],\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(0))

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{0}_{i}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle(

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[1],\hskip 9.24994pt\cdots,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[{\gamma-1}],

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[{\gamma}],\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle(F_{0}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[0],

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(1))

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{1}_{i}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle(

\displaystyle(F_{0}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[0],

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[1],\hskip 9.24994pt\cdots,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(2))

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle(

\displaystyle(F_{1}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[0],

\displaystyle 0,\hskip 9.24994pt\cdots,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[{\gamma-1}],

\displaystyle(F_{1}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[1],

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(3))

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{3}_{i}

Consider each vector in the above sequence, say,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{2}_{i}
. According to Observation
2
, we know

\displaystyle(

\displaystyle(F_{0}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[0],

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[1],\quad

\displaystyle\cdots,

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[{\gamma-1}],\quad

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[{\gamma}],

\displaystyle 0,\quad

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(2))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(

\displaystyle(F_{0}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[0],

\displaystyle(F_{0}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[1],

\displaystyle\cdots,

\displaystyle(F_{0}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[\gamma-1],\quad

\displaystyle(F_{0}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[\gamma],

\displaystyle 0,\quad

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(2))

More generally, it is easy to verify that every
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{2j}_{i}
is the concatenation of the vector
\left(F_{j-1}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}),0\right)
and
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(2j)
, and each
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{2j+1}_{i}
is a combination
SW_{j}\left(\left(F_{j-1}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}),0\right)\right)
and
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(2j+1)
, where
SW_{j}
is a one-to-one mapping that swaps two coordinates of a vector. A similar statement holds for
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{2j}_{k}
’s and
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{2j+1}_{k}
’s. Since
SW_{j}
’s,
F_{j}
’s and
\hat{F}_{j}
’s are all one-to-one mappings, and
\sigma^{\prime}
is an injection, each of the vectors in the sequence above is unique. More precisely, we have the following.

Lemma 9
.

For any
0\leq h\leq 2\gamma+1
and
1\leq i\leq n
,

•

If
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}_{i}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}
, then
h=h^{\prime}
and
i=i^{\prime}
;

•

If
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{h}_{i}=\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{h^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}
, then
h=h^{\prime}
and
i=i^{\prime}
.

Furthermore, by the fact that
F_{\gamma}=\hat{F}_{\gamma}\circ\hat{\tau}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{k}=\hat{\tau}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i})
, we have the following observation:

Observation 3
.

{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{2\gamma+2}_{i}=\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{2\gamma+2}_{k}.

Next, we consider any two adjacent vectors in the above sequence. We observe that they differ at exactly three positions – the last coordinate (i.e.,
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(j)
’s), and other two coordinates such that one of the two vectors has
0
coordinate. Other coordinates, e.g.,
(F_{0}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[0]
in
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{2}_{i}
and
(F_{1}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}))[0]
in
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{3}_{i}
are identical according to Observation
2
. This leads to the following Lemma.

Lemma 10
.

For any
0\leq h\leq 2\gamma+1
and
1\leq i\leq n
,

•

If
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}_{i}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h+1}_{i}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h^{\prime\prime}}_{i^{\prime\prime}}
where
h^{\prime}\leq h^{\prime\prime}
, then
h^{\prime}=h
,
h^{\prime\prime}=h+1
,
i^{\prime}=i^{\prime\prime}=i
;

•

If
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{h}_{i}+\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{h+1}_{i}=\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{h^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}+\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{h^{\prime\prime}}_{i^{\prime\prime}}
where
h^{\prime}\leq h^{\prime\prime}
, then
h^{\prime}=h
,
h^{\prime\prime}=h+1
,
i^{\prime}=i^{\prime\prime}=i
.

Proof.

We prove the first statement, that is,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}_{i}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h+1}_{i}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h^{\prime\prime}}_{i^{\prime\prime}}
implies
h^{\prime}=h
,
h^{\prime\prime}=h+1
,
i^{\prime}=i^{\prime\prime}=i
. The second statement can be proved in the same way.

We first consider the last coordinate of the summation
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}_{i}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h+1}_{i}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h^{\prime}}_{i^{\prime}}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h^{\prime\prime}}_{i^{\prime\prime}}
, which is
\sigma^{\prime}(h)+\sigma^{\prime}(h+1)=\sigma^{\prime}(h^{\prime})+\sigma^{\prime}(h^{\prime\prime})
. If
h=0
, according to
Property 3
of Lemma
6
,
1\times\sigma^{\prime}(1)
can only be expressed as
0+\sigma^{\prime}(1)
, hence we have
h^{\prime}=0
and
h^{\prime\prime}=1
. If
h\geq 1
, according to
Property 4
of Lemma
6
, we have
h^{\prime}=h
and
h^{\prime\prime}=h+1
.

Consider other coordinates of the equation
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}_{i}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h+1}_{i}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}_{i^{\prime}}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h+1}_{i^{\prime\prime}}
. On the left-side it is either
a
or
2a
where
a\in{\cal{S}}_{d}
. Consider the equation
a=b+c
where
b,c\in\{0\}\cup{\cal{S}}_{d}
. By Lemma
5
, there do not exist two numbers in
{\cal{S}}
that add up to
a
, hence we know
b,c\in\{0,a\}
. Similarly if
2a=b+c
for
a\in{\cal{S}}_{d}
and
b,c\in\{0\}\cup{\cal{S}}_{d}
, then
b
and
c
are both nonzero, for otherwise the linear equation
2y=y_{1}
admits a solution, which is a contradiction to that
{\cal{S}}_{d}
satisfies Lemma
5
. Hence,
2a=b+c
for
a,b,c\in{\cal{S}}_{d}
, and again by Lemma
5
we have
b=c=a
. Hence, we conclude that each coordinate of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}_{i^{\prime}}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h+1}_{i^{\prime\prime}}
must be the same as
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}_{i}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h+1}_{i}
, respectively. By Lemma
9
, it follows that
i^{\prime}=i^{\prime\prime}=i
.
∎

Using the same argument, we know if
2{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}_{i}^{2\gamma+2}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}_{i^{\prime}}^{h^{\prime}}+\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{h^{\prime\prime}}
, then
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}_{i}^{2\gamma+2}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}_{i^{\prime}}^{h^{\prime}}=\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{h^{\prime\prime}}
. Thus the following is also true.

Lemma 11
.

2{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}_{i}^{2\gamma+2}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}_{i^{\prime}}^{h^{\prime}}+\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{h^{\prime\prime}}
, then
h^{\prime}=h^{\prime\prime}=2\gamma+2
,
(i^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime})=(i,\tau(i))
.

We are now ready to prove Lemma
7
.

Proof of Lemma
7
.

The sequence of
E_{i}
that links
\sigma(i)
and
\sigma(k)
for
k=\tau(i)
is exactly
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{0}_{i}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{1}_{i},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{2}_{i},\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{2\gamma+2}_{i}=\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{2\gamma+2}_{k}
,
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{2\gamma+1}_{k}
,
\cdots
,
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{1}_{k},\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{0}_{k}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{k}
(recall that by a vector
\textstyle\bf b
we mean the integer
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}
with
x=5d+1=O(\log n)
). The uniqueness of each linking sequence is ensured by Lemma
9
and Lemma
10
. The largest number is bounded by
x^{\gamma+5}=n^{1+O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log\log n}})}
. Furthermore,
\omega=O(\gamma)=O(\frac{\log n}{\log\log n})
. Thus, all properties of Lemma
7
are satisfied.
∎

Appendix A
Omitted Proofs in Section
2
- Algorithms AL
1
, AL

, AL

A.1
Algorithm 1

Lemma 12
.

Consider an instance after the preprocessing of Lemma
1
. For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists an algorithm AL
1
that returns an
(1+O(\epsilon))
-approximate solution for
P||\sum_{i}C_{i}^{q}
and runs in time
(m/\epsilon)^{O(m)}
.

The algorithm can be formulated as a dynamic program. For each
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, we create a set of states
\mathcal{F}_{h}
. A state
(h,L_{1},\dots,L_{m})
belongs to
\mathcal{F}_{h}
if it is possible to assign jobs
1,\dots,h
on machines such that the total load on machine
i
equals
L_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}
. Starting from
(0,\dots,0)\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
, every state in
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
can give rise to some states in
\mathcal{F}_{i}
by trying all the possible assignment of job
h
. And the solution is given by the state in
\mathcal{F}_{n}
with the minimum objective i.e.,
\sum_{i=1}^{m}L_{i}^{q}
. Due to Lemma
1
, we know that one of the optimal solutions has a load vector
({L_{1}^{*}},\dots,{L_{m}^{*}})\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
satisfying
L_{i}^{*}\leq 2
for all
i
. Clearly, the running time of Algorithm
1
is
O(m\sum_{i}|\mathcal{F}_{i}|)
. However, the total number of states stored during the dynamic programming can be pseudo-polynomial. Fortunately, using the framework by Woeginger
[
41
]
, we are able to trim the state space to make it polynomial. More precisely, for any
\delta>0
construct
\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
satisfying the following property:

•

the size of each
\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
is bounded by
(1/\delta)^{O(m)}
;

•

for each
(i,L_{1},\dots,L_{m})\in\mathcal{F}_{i}
, there exists
(i,\hat{L}_{1},\dots,\hat{L}_{m})\in\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
such that
L_{j}\leq\hat{L}_{j}\leq L_{j}(1+\delta)^{i}
.

In Algorithm
1
, we set the parameter
\delta=\epsilon/n
. Due to Lemma
1
,
n\in O(m/\epsilon)
, the total running time is bounded by
(m/\epsilon)^{O(m)}
. Let
(n,\hat{L}_{1},\dots,\hat{L}_{m})
be the state with the minimum objective in
\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
, and
(n,L_{1}^{*},\dots,L_{m}^{*})
be the state with the minimum objective in
\mathcal{F}_{n}
. Hence,
(n,\hat{L}_{1},\dots,\hat{L}_{m})
is an
(1+O(\epsilon))
-approximate solution, as,

\sum_{i}\hat{L}_{i}^{q}\leq(1+\delta)^{nq}\sum_{i}{L_{i}^{*}}^{q}\leq(1+2q\epsilon)OPT,

where
q
is a fixed constant.

A.2
Algorithm 2

We first recall Lemma
2
and then present its proof.

Lemma
2
.

Consider an instance after the preprocessing of Lemma
1
. For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists an algorithm AL
2
that outputs an
(1+O(\epsilon))
-approximation solution for
P||\sum_{i}C_{i}^{q}
within
m^{\tilde{O}(1/\sqrt{\epsilon})}
time.

Proof.

Based on Lemma
3
, we design AL
2
as a dynamic program as follows.

We say that a vector
(i,v,u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{\tau})
is a valid state if it is possible to assign the
u_{h}
largest jobs in
\mathcal{G}_{h}
, for each
h
, on the first
i
machines with the objective equal to
v
. In our algorithm, for each
i\in\{0,\ldots,m\}
, we construct a set
\mathcal{F}_{i}
of valid states. Start from
(0,\dots,0)\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
. To construct the valid states in
\mathcal{F}_{i}
, we consider a state in
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
and try all possible assignments of jobs to machine
i
that respect the ordering of jobs given by Lemma
3
and the load bound for each machine implied by Lemma
1
. Given the sets
\mathcal{F}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{m}
, the answer can be found by searching the state with the minimum objective in
\mathcal{F}_{m}
. In order to limit the number of states, we can eliminate
dominated
states. Namely, if two states
(i,v,u_{1},\dots,u_{\tau})
and
(i,v^{\prime},u_{1},\dots,u_{\tau})
in
\mathcal{F}_{i}
satisfy
v^{\prime}<v
, then we say that
(i,v^{\prime},u_{1},\dots,u_{\tau})
is dominated and delete it from
\mathcal{F}_{i}
.

The overall running time of AL
2
can be bounded as follows. Recall that by Lemma
1
,
p_{j}\geq\epsilon
and that the load of each machine is at most 2. Hence, each state in
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
can give rise to at most
(2/\epsilon+1)^{\tau}
(dominated or undominated) states in
\mathcal{F}_{i}
. As also the number of jobs in each set
\mathcal{G}_{h}
is bounded by
2m/\epsilon
, the number of undominated states in
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is at most
(2m/\epsilon+1)^{\tau}=m^{\tilde{O}(\sqrt{1/\epsilon})}
. Hence, each set
\mathcal{F}_{i}
can be constructed in time
(2/\epsilon+1)^{\tau}m^{\tilde{O}(\sqrt{1/\epsilon})}=m^{\tilde{O}(\sqrt{1/\epsilon})}
, which implies the same bound for the overall running of our dynamic programming algorithm. The lemma follows.
∎

A.3
Algorithm 3

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 13
.

Consider an instance after the preprocessing of Lemma
1
. For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists an algorithm that outputs a feasible schedule for well structured instance of
P||\sum_{i}C_{i}^{q}
whose objective value is at most
OPT+O(\log^{2}m)
within
(1/\epsilon)^{O(1)}+n^{O(1)}
time.

Recall Lemma
1
, and that an approximation scheme for well structured instances also implies an approximation scheme for general instances. Given Lemma
13
and the fact that
OPT\geq m
for well structured instances, we know that the additive error
O(\log^{2}m)\leq\epsilon OPT
if
m=\Omega(1/\epsilon\log^{2}(1/\epsilon))
. Hence, Theorem
1
is proved.

In the following, we present Algorithm
3
, which is the algorithm claimed in Lemma
13
. Algorithm
3
modifies upon the famous algorithm for bin packing by Karmarkar and Karp
[
23
]
. Given an instance
I
,
m(I)
denotes the number of machines, and
\text{size}(I)
denotes the total processing time of jobs. To exclude the trivial cases, if
I
consists of less than
m(I)
jobs, then it is obvious that the optimal solution assigns each job to a separate machine. Hence, in the following parts, we can assume without of loss generality there are more than
m(I)
jobs.

We give a very high-level description. The scheduling problem can be interpreted as a bin packing problem where the bin number is a constraint, and the objective is to minimize the cost of bins instead of minimizing the number of bins (where the cost of a bin is its load to the power of
q
). Under such an interpretation, we are able to iteratively apply the
harmonic grouping scheme
to round job processing times and establish a
configuration LP
for the rounded instance. Based on the extreme point solution of the configuration LP, we assign jobs to roughly
m(I)/2
machines and continue with the remaining jobs and machines.

As discussed in Lemma
1
, here we assume the processing time of all jobs in
I
are within
[\epsilon,1]
. Suppose there are
\chi
distinct job processing times with
b_{1}
jobs of processing time
p_{1}
,
b_{2}
jobs of processing time
b_{2}
, … ,
b_{\chi}
jobs of processing time
p_{\chi}
. Consider the the subset of jobs that can be scheduled on a single machine, which can be characterized by a
\chi
-tuple
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}=(t_{1j},t_{2j},\cdots,t_{\chi j})
where
t_{ij}
indicates the number of jobs of processing time
p_{i}
on this machine. We call any
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}
with
t_{ij}\leq b_{i}
for every
i
as a configuration. Let
N
denote the number of configurations, let
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{2},\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{N}
be a complete enumeration of them.

We establish a configuration integer program for instance
I
as follows. We introduce a variable
x_{j}
for each configuration
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}
which indicates the number of machines which is scheduled according to
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}
. Consequently, all machines of configuration
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}
accommodate
t_{ij}x_{j}
jobs of processing time
p_{i}
. We define the load, or total processing time of configuration
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}
as
L({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{\chi}t_{ij}p_{i}
. We define the cost of configuration
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}
as
v_{j}=(\sum_{i=1}^{\chi}t_{ij}p_{i})^{q}
.

\displaystyle\text{Conf-IP($I$)}:

\displaystyle\min

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}{v}_{j}x_{j}

(4a)

\displaystyle s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}x_{j}=m(I)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}t_{ij}x_{j}\geq b_{i},\quad i=1,2,\cdots,\chi

(4b)

\displaystyle x_{j}\in\mathbb{N},\quad j=1,2,\cdots,N

Let
{OPT}_{IP}(I)
be the optimal objective value of Conf-IP(
I
).
Relaxing the integral constraint
x_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
to
x_{j}\geq 0
in Conf-LP(
{I}
), we obtain a configuration linear programming Conf-LP(
{I}
). Let
OPT_{LP}({I})
be its optimal objective value, it is obvious that
OPT_{LP}({I})\leq OPT_{IP}({I})
. We have the following lemma.

Lemma 14
.

There exists an algorithm of running time polynomial in
\chi,\log(m/\epsilon)
and
1/\varsigma
, and returns a feasible extreme point solution of objective value at most
OPT_{LP}({I})+\varsigma
for Conf-LP(
{I}
).

Proof.

We consider the dual of Conf-LP(
{I}
):

\displaystyle\text{Dual-LP(${I}$)}:

\displaystyle\max

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\chi}b_{i}y_{i}-m(I)z

(5a)

\displaystyle s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\chi}t_{ij}y_{i}-z\leq{v}_{j},\quad j=1,2,\cdots,N

\displaystyle y_{i}\geq 0,\quad j=1,2,\cdots,N

We use a similar algorithm as that for the classical bin packing problem. We employ the ellipsoid method to solve Dual-LP(
\bar{I}
). We give a brief description. The ellipsoid method iteratively computes a sequence of ellipsoids
E_{0},E_{1},\cdots,
. In each iteration, it implements a separation oracle to check whether the center of the current ellipsoid
E_{k}
, say,
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k},z^{k})=(y_{1}^{k},\cdots,y_{\chi}^{k},z^{k})
, is feasible. If it is, then it outputs a cut
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}-m(I)z\geq{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k}-m(I)z^{k}
where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}=(b_{1},\cdots,b_{\chi})
; Otherwise, it finds out a violating constraint, say,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}-z\leq\bar{v}_{j}
, and outputs
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}-z\leq{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k}-z^{k}
. Incorporating the cut output by the separation oracle, the ellipsoid method computes a new ellipsoid
E_{k+1}
and guarantees that the volume of the new ellipsoid is smaller than
E_{k}
by a factor of
e^{-\frac{1}{5(\chi+1)}}
. After a polynomial number of iterations (specifically, which is polynomial in
\log 1/\varsigma
), the ellipsoid method finds a near-optimal feasible solution with an additive error of
\varsigma
.

In their seminal work, Karmarkar and Karp
[
23
]
further prove that to compute an approximate solution to Conf-LP(
{I}
) up to an additive precision of
\varsigma
, it suffices to construct an approximate separation oracle such that in each iteration, instead of checking whether the center
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k},z^{k})
is feasible and returns a violating constraint if it is infeasible, the approximate separation oracle checks a point
(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k},\tilde{z}^{k})
and does the following:

•

If
(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k},\tilde{z}^{k})
violates a constraint, say,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}\cdot\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k}-\tilde{z}^{k}>{v}_{j}
, then outputs cut
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}-{z}\leq{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k}-\tilde{z}^{k}
;

•

If
(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k},\tilde{z}^{k})
does not violate any constraint, then outputs

{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}\cdot{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}-m(I)z\geq{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}\cdot\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k}-m(I)\tilde{z}^{k}
.

Karmarkar and Karp showed that ellipsoid method equipped with the approximate separation oracle can return a near-optimal solution within an additive error of
O(\varsigma)
as long as the point
(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k},\tilde{z}^{k})
in each iteration satisfies that

•

For any constraint, if
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}\cdot\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k}-\tilde{z}^{k}>{v}_{j}
, then
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}\cdot{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k}-{z}^{k}>{v}_{j}
;

•

For the objective function,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k}-m(I)z^{k}\leq{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}\cdot\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k}-m(I)\tilde{z}^{k}+\varsigma
.

Here the first property ensures that if
(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k},\tilde{z}^{k})
is infeasible, then
({{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k},{z}^{k})
is also infeasible by violating the same constraint, and therefore the approximate separation oracle proceeds exactly the same as an (accurate) separation oracle. The second property ensures that the approximate separation oracle will never cut off a feasible point whose objective value is significantly better than
(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k},\tilde{z}^{k})
by
\varsigma
, and hence ensures the near-optimality.

Now we describe our approximate separation oracle as follows. We first round
v_{j}

up
to be the nearest value of the form
(1+\epsilon)^{k}
and let it be
\bar{v}_{j}
. Notice that there are a polynomial number of distinct rounded values. Given an arbitrary point
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k},z^{k})
, we consider all inequalities of the form

{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k}\leq\bar{v}_{j}+z^{k}.

Our goal is to find out a violating constraint or determine there is none. Since there are only a polynomial number of different values for
\bar{v}_{j}
’s, we can sequentially check for every value
(1+\epsilon)^{h}
, whether
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k},z^{k})
violates the constraint
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k}\leq(1+\epsilon)^{h}+z^{k}
for all configurations
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}
such that
(1+\epsilon)^{h-1}<({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf p$}}})^{q}\leq(1+\epsilon)^{h}
where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf p$}}}=(p_{1},\cdots,p_{\chi})
. We argue that we can drop the lower bound by sequentially checking for every value
(1+\epsilon)^{h}
, whether
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k},z^{k})
violates the constraint
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k}\leq(1+\epsilon)^{h}+z^{k}
for all configurations
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}
such that
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf p$}}})^{q}\leq(1+\epsilon)^{h}
.
This is because that if
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k},z^{k})
violates
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k}\leq(1+\epsilon)^{h}+z^{k}
but
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf p$}}})^{q}\leq(1+\epsilon)^{h-1}
, say,
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf p$}}})^{q}
rounded up to
(1+\epsilon)^{h^{\prime}}
for
h^{\prime}<h
, then
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k},z^{k})
also violates
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k}\leq(1+\epsilon)^{h^{\prime}}+z^{k}
, which will be found out already. Hence, finding a violating constraint is equivalent as finding a vector
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}\leq{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}
such that
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf p$}}})^{q}\leq(1+\epsilon)^{h}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k}
is maximized, and comparing this maximal value with
z^{k}+(1+\epsilon)^{k}
. This is a knapsack problem which admits a fully polynomial time approximation scheme (FPTAS). More precisely, using the same method as Karmarkar and Karp
[
23
]
, we can round
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k}
to some value
\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k}
close enough such that

•

For any
\chi
-dimensional vector
\textstyle\bf d
whose coordinates are non-negative and
\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}\|_{1}\leq n
,
0\leq{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}\cdot{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}\cdot\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k}\leq\varsigma
;

•

In polynomial time (specifically, polynomial in
1/\varsigma
), we are able to find
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}^{*}
such that by taking
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}^{*}
,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}\cdot\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k}
is maximized subject to
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf p$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf p$}}})^{q}\leq(1+\epsilon)^{h}
.

Overall, our above argument ensures that in polynomial time we either determine some configuration
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}
such that
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k}>\bar{v}_{j}+z^{k}
for some
j
, and hence

{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k}+z^{k}\geq{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}_{j}\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k}\bar{v}_{j}+z^{k}>\bar{v}_{j}+z^{k}\geq v_{j}+z^{k};

or we conclude there is no such configuration and guarantee that
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}^{k}-m(I)z^{k}\leq{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}\cdot\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}}^{k}-m(I)\tilde{z}^{k}+\varsigma
.

Hence, there exists an approximate separation oracle for Dual-LP(
I
), indicating that Dual-LP(
I
) can be solved using the ellipsoid method in polynomial time (up to arbitrary precision). Notice that the derived solution may not necessarily be an extreme point, however, by using exactly the same argument as that of Karmarkar and Karp
[
23
]
, we can make it into an extreme point solution.
∎

Consider the near-optimal extreme point solution
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I)
given by Lemma
14
. We denote by
\lfloor{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I)\rfloor=(\lfloor x_{1}(I)\rfloor,\cdots,\lfloor x_{N}(I)\rfloor)
the rounded solution. Similar as the algorithm for bin packing, we assign jobs to machine according to
\lfloor{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I)\rfloor
and then proceed with the residue instance
I_{res}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}})
. Denote by
I_{res}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}})
the
residue instance
where we take away jobs scheduled according to
\lfloor{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I)\rfloor
from the original instance
I
, i.e.,
I_{res}
consists jobs in
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I)-\lfloor{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I)\rfloor
. It is easy to see
I_{res}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}})
consists of
m(I_{res}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}))=m(I)-\sum_{j}\lfloor x_{j}(I)\rfloor
machines.

Lemma 15
.

OPT_{LP}(I_{res}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}))+OPT_{LP}(I\setminus I_{res}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}))\leq OPT_{LP}(I)

Proof.

We know each configuration of instance
I
is also a configuration of instance
I_{res}
and
I\setminus I_{res}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}})
. Hence, for any solution
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I)
of instance
I
,
\lfloor{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I)\rfloor
is an feasible solution of
I\setminus I_{res}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}})
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I)-\lfloor{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I)\rfloor
is an feasible solution of
I_{res}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}})
.
∎

Similar to the bin packing algorithm by Karmarkar and Karp
[
23
]
, we will employ the
harmonic grouping scheme
to round the instance and apply Lemma
14
on the rounded instance. It has to be noticed that the processing time of every job in the instance is no more than 1 which is guaranteed by Lemma
1
. The harmonic grouping works as follows: We deal with the job one by one in non-decreasing order of its processing time and pack the job into the current group. At any time, only one group is open. When the total processing time of jobs in the current group is at least 2, we close it and start a new group. By doing this, all jobs in
I
are packed into
r
groups i.e.,
G_{1},\dots,G_{r}
. We discard all jobs in
G_{1}
along with
|G_{i-1}|-|G_{i}|
jobs with smallest processing time in
G_{i}
for each
i\in[2,r]
and let it be
I_{d}
. For those remaining jobs, we lift the processing time to the largest one among their group and let it be
I^{\prime}
. The
harmonic grouping scheme
has the following property
[
40
]
:

•

the number of distinct job processing times in
I^{\prime}
is at most
\text{size}(I)/2
;

•

\text{size}(I_{d})\in O(\log\text{size}(I))
.

Lemma 16
.

OPT_{LP}(I^{\prime})\leq OPT_{LP}(I)

Proof.

Given any solution
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I)
of instance
I
, for each configuration we can replace each job
j
in group
G_{i}
with a job
j^{\prime}
in group
G_{i+1}
. In such a modified solution, all jobs of
I^{\prime}
are scheduled. Since
p_{j^{\prime}}\leq p_{j}
holds, the objective does not increase.
∎

Now we formally present Algorithm
3
. Given an instance
I
of the scheduling problem, we first apply the harmonic grouping scheme to obtain the rounded instance
I^{\prime}
along with another instance
I_{d}
composed by the discarded jobs. Then we apply Lemma
14
to derive a feasible solution
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I^{\prime})
for
I^{\prime}
and assign jobs to machines according to
\lfloor{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I^{\prime})\rfloor
and close these machines. The remaining jobs
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I^{\prime})-\lfloor{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I^{\prime})\rfloor
and the remaining empty machines
m(I^{\prime})-\|\lfloor{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}(I^{\prime}_{j})\rfloor\|_{1}
(here
\|\cdot\|_{1}
is the 1-norm, which counts the number of integral configurations) forms a new instance
I^{\prime}_{res}
. In the next iteration,
I^{\prime}_{res}
servers as the input and we repeat this process until there are only constant machines left. For the instance with constant machines, we call Algorithm
1
. Then we do the balancing operation to make sure that for every two machines their load difference is at most 1. At last, we group all the discarded jobs into
O(\log^{2}m)
groups where the total processing time of each group is at most 2. This can be easily done, since the processing time of each job is at most 1. We pick arbitrarily
O(\log^{2}m)
machines and schedule each group of jobs on one machine.

Algorithm 3

Pseudo Code Description of AL
3

1:

I_{D}=\emptyset

2:

while

m(I)>10

do

3:

apply harmonic grouping scheme to create instance
I^{\prime}
and
I_{d}

4:

I_{D}=I_{D}\cup I_{d}

5:

let
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}}(I^{\prime})
be the
(\epsilon/\log m)
-balanced solution for Conf-IP(
I^{\prime}
)

6:

schedule
I^{\prime}\setminus I^{\prime}_{res}(\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}})
according to
\lfloor\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}}(I^{\prime})\rfloor

7:

I=I^{\prime}_{res}(\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}})

8:

end

while

9:

schedule
I
using Algorithm
1

10:

while

\exists i,j
s.t.
L_{i}-L_{j}>1

do

11:

move the job with the largest processing time in machine
i
to machine
j

12:

end

while

13:

schedule
I_{D}
on arbitrarily
O(\log^{2}m)
machines with max increasing load 2

Finally, we estimate the overall loss incurred. In each iteration, only
m(I)/2+1
variables take non-zero value in solution
I^{\prime}_{res}(\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}})
. Hence,
m(I^{\prime}_{res}(\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}}))\leq m(I)/2+1
and after at most
O(\log m)
rounds Algorithm
3
terminates. Each iteration introduces an additional cost of
\varsigma
, together with discarded jobs of total processing time
O(\log^{2}m)
, which need to be handled at last. Set
\varsigma=O(\log m)
and observing that
OPT\geq OPT_{LP}(I)
and
OPT\geq m
, we know the overall additional cost is bounded by
O(\log^{2}m)
. Now consider all the discarded jobs. Through Lemma
1
and the balancing operation, we know the load of machines in the solution is at most 3. Meanwhile, due to the convexity of the objective, the balancing operation does not increase the objective value. Given that
q
is a constant, hence, the overall objective value increases by at most
O(\log^{2}m)
, and Lemma
13
is proved.

Appendix B
Omitted Proofs in Section
3.1
- Proof of Lemma
4

The goal of this section is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma
4
.

Assuming ETH, there exists a constant
\beta\in(0,1)
such that for any sufficiently small
\epsilon^{\prime},\delta>0
, it is not possible to distinguish between instances of 3SAT
\,{}^{\prime}
with
(1-\epsilon^{\prime})\cdot 4n/3
clauses where at least
4n/3
clauses are satisfiable, from instances where at most
(\beta+\epsilon^{\prime})\cdot 4n/3
clauses are satisfiable, in time
2^{O(n^{1-\delta})}
.

Towards the proof, we start with the following result (see, e.g., Corollary 1 of
[
5
]
).

Lemma 17
.

[
34
,
5
]

Under ETH, for sufficiently small
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, and
\delta>0
, it is impossible to distinguish between instances of 3SAT with
\Lambda
clauses where at least
(1-\epsilon^{\prime})\Lambda
are satisfiable from instances where at most
(7/8+\epsilon^{\prime})\Lambda
are satisfiable, in time
O(2^{\Lambda^{1-\delta}})
.

Applying the classical technique of constructing enforcer via expander for 3SAT (see, e.g., Theorem 5 of
[
39
]
), we have the following,

Lemma 18
.

[
39
]

There exists a constant
d_{0}
such that given a 3SAT formula
