\phi
with
\Lambda
clauses, another 3SAT formula
\phi^{\prime}
with
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda+3d_{0}\Lambda=O(\Lambda)
clauses can be constructed in polynomial time such that:

•

Every variable occurs in at most
2d+1
clauses in
\phi^{\prime}
;

•

There is an assignment for
\phi
where at most
k
clauses are not satisfied if and only if there is an assignment for
\phi^{\prime}
such that at most
k
clauses are not satisfied.

Denote by 3SAT-
d
the 3SAT problem where every variable occurs at most
d
times. Combining Lemma
17
and Lemma
18
, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 19
.

Under ETH, there exists some constants
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that for sufficiently small
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, and
\delta>0
, it is impossible to distinguish between instances of 3SAT-
d
with
\Lambda
clauses where at least
(1-\epsilon^{\prime})\Lambda
are satisfiable from instances where at most
(\alpha+\epsilon^{\prime})\Lambda
are satisfiable, in time
O(2^{\Lambda^{1-\delta}})
.

It is worth mentioning that the reduction in
[
39
]
involves constructing 2-clauses, that is, 3SAT-
d
in Lemma
19
refers to a 3SAT instance where clauses may contain 2 or 3 variables. For ease of presentation, we want to enforce every clause to contain exactly 3 variables
1
1

1

We remark, however, that our reduction also works if
C_{1}
contains 2-causes and 3-clauses. It suffices to create two CL
ℓ
, one true copy and one false copy instead of three, and meanwhile adjust the number of dummy jobs.
. This can be done by introducing dummy variables together with 3-clauses that enforce a dummy variable to be true or false (called enforcers). In particular, Berman et al.
[
4
]
provide a general enforcer that allows them to deduce the APX-hardness of MAX3SAT (where every clause contains 3 variables and every variable appears 4 times) through the APX-hardness of MAX2SAT. We can apply their technique directly to get a strengthened version of Lemma
19
where in 3SAT-
d
every clause contains exactly 3 variables.

The following proof is a slight variation of that from Tovey
[
38
]
.

Lemma 20
.

Given a 3SAT-
d
formula
\phi
with
\Lambda
clauses, a 3SAT
\,{}^{\prime}
formula
\phi^{\prime}
with
|C_{1}|=\Lambda
and
|C_{2}|\leq 3\Lambda
clauses can be constructed in polynomial time such that:

•

If there is an assignment for
\phi
where at most
k
clauses are not satisfied, then there is an assignment for
\phi^{\prime}
where at most
k
clauses are not satisfied.

•

If there is an assignment for
\phi^{\prime}
where there are at most
k
clauses not satisfied, then there is an assignment for
\phi
where at most
kd
clauses are not satisfied.

Proof.

Let
z
be any variable in
\phi
and suppose it appears
\ell\leq d

times in clauses. If
\ell=1
then we add a dummy clause
(z\oplus\neg z)
. Otherwise
\ell\geq 2
and we introduce
\ell
new variables
z_{1}
,

z_{2}
,
\cdots
,
z_{\ell}
and
\ell
new clauses
(z_{1}\oplus\neg z_{2})
,

(z_{2}\oplus\neg z_{3})
,
\cdots
,
(z_{\ell}\oplus\neg z_{1})
which enforce
z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{\ell}
to take the same truth value. Meanwhile
we replace the
\ell
occurrences of
z
in the original clauses by
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
,
\cdots
,

z_{\ell}
in turn and remove
z
. By doing so we transform
\phi

into a new formula
\phi^{\prime}
by introducing at most
3\Lambda
new variables and
3\Lambda

new clauses.

Notice that each new clause we add in
\phi^{\prime}
is of the form

(z_{i}\oplus\neg z_{i^{\prime}})
. We let
C_{2}
be the set of them and let
C_{1}

be the set of other clauses. It is easy to verify that
\phi^{\prime}
is
an instance of 3SAT
′
. Notice that every clause in
C_{1}
has a corresponding clause in
\phi
by replacing
z_{i}
’s with
z
.

Suppose there is an assignment for
\phi
where at most
k
clauses are not satisfied. Then for any variable
z
in
\phi
that occurs
\ell
times, we let
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
,
\cdots
,
z_{\ell}
all take the same value as
z
. It is easy to see that at most
k
clauses in
C_{1}
of
\phi^{\prime}
are not satisfied.

Suppose there is an assignment for
\phi^{\prime}
where at most
k
clauses in
C_{1}
are not satisfied. For any variable
z
in
\phi
that correspond to
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
,
\cdots
,
z_{\ell}
in
\phi^{\prime}
, we let
z_{i}
take the same value of
z_{1}
for all
i
. Now we check the number of additional unsatisfied clauses in
C_{1}
we introduce by doing so. If all
z_{i}
’s take the same value, then no additional unsatisfied clauses are introduced. Otherwise, it is possible that some of the clauses in
C_{1}
, which is satisfied by
z_{2}
,
z_{3}
,
\cdots
or
z_{\ell}
, becomes unsatisfied. But there are at most
d-1
such kind of clauses. Hence, at most
d-1
unsatisfied clauses are introduced, if there is at least one unsatisfied clause among
(z_{1}\oplus\neg z_{2})
,
(z_{2}\oplus\neg z_{3})
,
\cdots
,
(z_{\ell}\oplus\neg z_{1})
. This implies that we have introduced at most
k(d-1)
unsatisfied clauses by setting
z_{i}=z_{1}
for all variables, i.e., there are at most
k(d-1)+k=kd
unsatisfied clauses in
C_{1}
now. Hence, there is an assignment for
\phi
where at most
kd
clauses are not satisfied.
∎

Given Lemma
20
, we know that if there is an assignment for
\phi
with are at most
\alpha\Lambda
unsatisfied clauses, then there is an assignment for
\phi^{\prime}
with every clause in
C_{2}
satisfied and at most
\alpha\Lambda
clauses in
C_{1}
unsatisfied; if every assignment has at least
\beta\Lambda
unsatisfied clauses in
\phi
, then there are at least
\beta\Lambda/d
unsatisfied clauses in
\phi^{\prime}
. Hence, according to Lemma
19
, Lemma
4
is proved.

Appendix C
Omitted Contents in Section
3.2
- Why Old Reduction does not Work

Chen et al.
[
7
]
provided a reduction that meets the conditions CO1 to CO4 with job processing times, and hence the target value
T
, being
O(n^{1+\delta})
for any arbitrary small constant
\delta>0
. This reduction provides a strong lower bound for
P||C_{max}
, but does not work well for our problem
P||\sum_{i}C_{i}^{q}
. To see this, we take
q=2
as an example and compare the two objective values for the constructed scheduling problem when
I_{sat}
is satisfiable and when it is not. If
I_{sat}
is satisfiable, then there exists a schedule such that every machine has a load of exactly
T
, implying that the optimal objective value is
mT^{2}
. Otherwise, at least one machine has load
T+1
or more and machine has load
T-1
or less, and then the optimal objective is at least
(m-2)T^{2}+(T+1)^{2}+(T-1)^{2}=mT^{2}+2
. For the sake of contradiction, let us assume that there exists a PTAS with running time
2^{O((1/\epsilon)^{\kappa})}
for some
\kappa\in(0,1)
. If we take
\epsilon
to be sufficiently small such that
mT^{2}\epsilon\leq 1
, then the PTAS can be used to determine whether the constructed scheduling instance admits a feasible schedule of objective value at most
mT^{2}+1<mT^{2}+2
, and hence whether
I_{sat}
is satisfiable. The running time of the PTAS becomes
2^{O((1/\epsilon)^{\kappa})}=2^{O((mT^{2})^{\kappa})}
. Plug in
m=O(n)
and
T=O(n^{1+\delta})
in the reduction, we have
mT^{2}=O(n^{3+2\delta})
. Hence, if
\kappa=1/3-\delta
, we have
(mT^{2})^{\kappa}\leq O(n^{1-\delta})
, and an efficient PTAS of running time
2^{O((1/\epsilon)^{1/3-\delta})}
can thus determine the satisfiability of
I_{sat}
in
2^{O(n^{1-\delta})}
time, contradicting ETH. To summarize, the above argument implies a lower bound of
2^{O((1/\epsilon)^{1/3-\delta})}
on the running time of PTAS for arbitrary constant
\delta>0
, which is not strong enough to match our algorithms in Theorem
1
.

To overcome the obstacle, a natural idea is to decrease the value of
m
or
T
in the reduction. However, if, say,
m=n^{0.9}
and
T=n^{O(1)}
, then we know the standard dynamic programming for scheduling returns the optimal solution in
T^{O(m)}=2^{O(n^{0.9})}
time; similarly, if
T=n^{0.9}
and
m=n^{O(1)}
, then we know there are at most
n^{0.9}
different kinds of jobs, and the scheduling problem can also be solved in time
2^{O(n^{0.9})}
through dynamic programming. Hence, we cannot expect to reduce
I_{sat}
to such scheduling instances, assuming ETH.

As a consequence, in this paper, we will not try to decrease
m
or
T
. Instead, we increase the gap between the two optimal objective values for the constructed scheduling problem when
I_{sat}
is satisfiable and when
I_{sat}
is not satisfiable by exploiting the hardness gap in Lemma
4
.

Appendix D
Omitted Proofs in Section
3.4
- Proof of Lemma
5

Lemma
5
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. There exists a subset
{\cal{S}}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{N}
such that
|{\cal{S}}|\geq N^{1-c_{0}\sqrt{\frac{1}{{\log N}}}}
for some sufficiently large
c_{0}
(in particular,
c_{0}=7
suffices), and for any
y\in{\cal{S}}
and
1\leq h\leq 5
, the linear equation
h\cdot y=y_{1}+y_{2}+\cdots+y_{h}
with
y_{i}\in{\cal{S}}
for all
i
has a unique solution
y_{1}=y_{2}=\cdots=y_{h}=y
.

Proof.

For any
d\geq 2
,
M\geq 2
and
k\leq(M+1)(d-1)^{2}
, we let
x=5d-1
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}=(1,x,x^{2},\cdots,x^{M})
,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}=({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[0],{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[1],\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[M])
. We define the set
S_{k}(M,d)
as:

\displaystyle{\cal{S}}_{k}(M,d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{y:y={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}+x^{M+1}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[0]+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[1]x^{1}+\cdots+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[M]x^{M}+x^{M+1},

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[i]\in\mathbb{N},0\leq{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[i]<d,\sum_{i=0}^{M}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[i])^{2}=k\}.

That is,
{\cal{S}}_{k}(M,d)
is the set of all integers which can be expressed in the form of
{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}}\cdot{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}}+x^{M+1}
such that
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[i]\in[0,d)
and
\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}\|_{2}^{2}=k
, where
\|\cdot\|_{2}
is the
\ell_{2}
-norm of a vector.

We claim that for any
y\in{\cal{S}}_{k}(M,d)
and
1\leq h,h^{\prime}\leq 5
, if
hy=y_{1}+y_{2}+\cdots+y_{h^{\prime}}
,
y_{i}\in S_{k}(M,d)
, then we have
h^{\prime}=h
,
y_{1}=y_{2}=\cdots=y_{h}=y
. Let
y={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}+x^{M+1}
and
y_{j}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}+x^{M+1}
, where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}=({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}[0],{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}[2],\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}[M])
.
Using the fact that
x=5d-1
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}[i]<d
, we know
for
h^{\prime}\leq 5
we have
\sum_{j=1}^{h^{\prime}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}[i]<x
. Hence by checking the coefficient of
x^{M+1}
on both sides of the equation
hy=\sum_{j=1}^{h^{\prime}}y_{j}
, we have
h=h^{\prime}
.
Moreover, we can conclude that the coefficient of
x^{i}
in the sum
y_{1}+y_{2}+\cdots+y_{h^{\prime}}
equals
\sum_{j=1}^{h^{\prime}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}[i]
, hence by comparing the coefficient of
x^{i}
on both sides, we have

\sum_{j=1}^{h}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}=h{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}.

By the definition of
{\cal{S}}_{k}(M,d)
, the followings are true:

\sum_{i=0}^{M}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}[i])^{2}=k,\quad\forall j,

and

\sum_{i=0}^{M}(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{h}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}[i]}{h})^{2}=\sum_{i=0}^{M}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[i])^{2}=k

Hence,

\displaystyle k=\sum_{i=0}^{M}(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{h}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}[i]}{h})^{2}=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{h}\sum_{i=0}^{M}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}[i])^{2}}{h}=\sum_{i=0}^{M}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{h}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}[i])^{2}}{h}.

(6)

According to the inequality between the quadratic mean and arithmetic mean, we know

\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{h}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}[i]}{h}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{h}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}[i])^{2}}{h},\quad\forall i

and the equality only holds when
c_{j}[i]
’s are identical for all
j
. Hence by Eq (
6
) we know
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}
, and consequently

y_{j}=y
for
1\leq j\leq h
. Hence, the claim is true.

It remains to select an appropriate
{\cal{S}}_{k}(M,d)
such that
{\cal{S}}_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{N}
and has a large cardinality. Towards this, we first observe that the largest number of
{\cal{S}}_{k}(M,d)
is bounded by
(5d-1)^{M+2}
. We shall select
d
and
M
such that
(5d-1)^{M+2}\leq N
. Notice that there are
d^{M+1}-1
different positive integer numbers which can be expressed as
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}+x^{M+1}
where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[i]\in[0,d)
. Furthermore
k=\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq(M+1)(d-1)^{2}
, hence there exists some
1\leq k^{*}\leq(M+1)(d-1)^{2}
such that

|{\cal{S}}_{k^{*}}({M,d})|\geq\frac{d^{M+1}-1}{(M+1)(d-1)^{2}}>\frac{d^{M-1}}{M+1}.

It remains to select
d
and
M
subject to
(5d-1)^{M+2}\leq N
such that
\frac{d^{M-1}}{M+1}
is large. Below all logarithms are taken with the base
e
. We pick
M=\lfloor\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{\log 5}}\rfloor-2
and
d=\lfloor\frac{e^{\sqrt{\log 5\cdot\log N}}}{5}\rfloor
. It is easy to see that
(M+2)\log(5d-1)\leq\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{\log 5}}\cdot\log e^{\sqrt{\log 5\cdot\log N}}=\log N
, hence
(5d-1)^{M+2}\leq N
. Furthermore,
for sufficiently large
N
(e.g.,
N>e^{10}
), we know
d\geq\frac{e^{\sqrt{\log 5\cdot\log N}}}{10}
, hence

\displaystyle\frac{d^{M-1}}{M+1}=e^{(M-1)\log d-\log(M+1)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{(\sqrt{\log N\cdot\log 5}-\log 10)(\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{\log 5}}-4)-\frac{1}{2}(\log\log N-\log\log 5)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{\log N\cdot(1+\frac{-4\sqrt{\log N\log 5}-\frac{\log 10}{\sqrt{\log 5}}\sqrt{\log N}-\Omega(\log\log N)}{\log N})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N^{1-\Omega(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log N}})}

Hence, Lemma
5
is proved. In particular, it is easy to verify that
4\sqrt{\log 5}+\frac{\log 10}{\sqrt{\log 5}}\leq 7
, hence the
\frac{d^{M-1}}{M+1}\geq N^{1-\frac{7}{\sqrt{\log N}}}
.
∎

Appendix E
Omitted proofs in Section
3.4
- Proof of Lemma
7

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following.

Lemma
8
.

For any
\pi\in Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
, there exist
h
-shufflers
f_{h},\hat{f}_{h}\in Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
for every
1\leq h\leq\upsilon
such that
F({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}})=\hat{F}(\pi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}))
for any
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}\in{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}
, where
F=f_{\upsilon-1}\circ f_{\upsilon-2}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}
and
\hat{F}=\hat{f}_{\upsilon-1}\circ\hat{f}_{\upsilon-2}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{f}_{0}
. Furthermore,
f_{h}
’s and
\hat{f}_{h}
’s can be constructed in time that is polynomial in
|{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}|
.

For any finite set
X
, we denote by
Aut(X)
the set of all one-to-one mapping from
X
to itself. For any
f_{1},f_{2}\in Aut(X)
, we denote by
f_{1}\circ f_{2}\in Aut(X)
the composition of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, i.e.,
f_{1}\circ f_{2}(x)=f_{1}(f_{2}(x))
for any
x\in X
. Note that
Aut(X)
is a symmetric group under composition. We denote by
f^{-1}\in Aut(X)
the inverse of
f\in Aut(X)
.

Let
{\cal{M}}
be an arbitrary finite set of cardinality
t
. Let
\upsilon\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Denote by
{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}
the set of all
\upsilon
-dimensional vectors whose entries belong to
{\cal{M}}
.

For any vector
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}\in{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}
, we denote by
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[h]\in{\cal{M}}
the
h
-th coordinate of
\textstyle\bf y
, and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[-h]\in{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon-1}
the vector obtained by removing the
h
-th coordinate from
\textstyle\bf y
.

For any
f\in Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
and
0\leq h\leq\upsilon-1
, we call
f
an
h
-shuffler if
\left(f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}})\right)[-h]={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[-h]
for all
y\in\cal{M}^{\upsilon}
, that is,

f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}})=f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[0],{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[1],\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[\upsilon-1])=({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[0],\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[h-1],z,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[h+1],\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}[\upsilon-1])),

for some
z\in{\cal{M}}
.

We prove the following lemma.

Lemma 21
.

For any
\pi\in Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
and
0\leq h\leq\upsilon-1
, there exist
h
-shufflers
f,\hat{f}\in Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
such that for any
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}\in{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}
,
\left(f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}})\right)[h]=\left(\hat{f}(\pi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}))\right)[h]
. Furthermore,
f
and
\hat{f}
can be constructed in time that is polynomial in
|{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}|
.

Figure 2:
Illustration of Lemma
21
for
M=\{1,2,3\}
,
\upsilon=4
,
h=1
(recall that vectors start with the
0
-th coordinate). Each vertex in
U
(i.e., the solid circle) is a 4-dimensional vector. Each mega-vertex in
\bar{U}
(i.e., the dotted circle) contains exactly 3 vertices. Each solid line between vertices represents the mapping
\pi
which is colored by one of 3 colors.

Briefly speaking,
f
and
\hat{f}
shuffles the
h
-coordinate of
\textstyle\bf y
and its image under
\pi
such that they become identical.

Now we are ready to prove Lemma
8
.

Proof of Lemma
8
.

For ease of presentation, we let
{\cal{M}}=\{1,2,\cdots,t\}
.
Note that
|{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}|=t^{\upsilon}
. Sort elements (vectors) of
{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}
in an arbitrary order and denote them by
\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{2},\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{t^{\upsilon}}\}
. We create a bipartite graph
G=(U\cup W,E)
to represent
\pi
as follows: Both
U
and
W
contain
t^{\upsilon}
vertices. Let
U=\{u_{1},u_{2},\cdots,u_{t^{\upsilon}}\}
and
W=\{w_{1},w_{2},\cdots,w_{t^{\upsilon}}\}
. There is an edge between
u_{i}
and
w_{j}
if and only if
\pi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{i})={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}
. Since
\pi
is one-to-one mapping,
G
is 1-regular.

As each
u_{i}
and
w_{i}
correspond to
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{i}
, we will slightly abuse notation and write
u_{i}[h]
or
u_{i}[-h]
to refer to
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{i}[h]
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{i}[-h]
.

Contraction.
We contract the graph
G
as follows. We partition
U
(or
W
) into
t^{\upsilon-1}
subsets such that
u_{i}
and
u_{j}
(or
w_{i}
and
w_{j}
) are in the same subset if and only if
u_{i}[-h]=u_{j}[-h]
(or
w_{i}[-h]=w_{j}[-h]
). Denote by
\bar{U}_{i}
(or
\bar{W}_{i}
),
1\leq i\leq t^{\upsilon-1}
, all the subsets in the partition of
U
(or
W
). It is clear that each
\bar{U}_{i}
(or
\bar{W}_{i}
) contains exactly
t
vertices from
U
(or
W
). We now contract all the
t
vertices in
\bar{U}_{i}
(or
\bar{W}_{i}
) into one mega-vertex, and denote this mega-vertex as
\bar{u}_{i}
(or
\bar{w}_{i}
). By doing so we generate parallel edges, that is, there are
\ell
parallel edges between each pair of mega-vertices
\bar{u}_{i}
and
\bar{w}_{j}
if there are
\ell
edges between vertices in
\bar{U}_{i}
and
\bar{W}_{j}
in the original graph
G
. We denote by
\psi
an arbitrary one-to-one mapping between a parallel edge (between mega-vertices
\bar{u}_{i}
and
\bar{w}_{j}
) and an edge in
G
(between some vertex in subset
\bar{U}_{i}
and some vertex in subset
\bar{W}_{j}
).
Denote by
\bar{G}=(\bar{U}\cup\bar{W},\bar{E})
the contracted graph. Given that
G
is
1
-regular and every mega-vertex contains exactly
t
vertices, we have the following observation:

Observation 4
.

The contracted graph
\bar{G}=(\bar{U}\cup\bar{W},\bar{E})
is a
t
-regular bipartite graph.

Coloring.
It is known that every bipartite regular graph admits a perfect matching (see, e.g.
[
26
]
). Consequently, every
t
-regular bipartite graph can be decomposed into
t
perfect matchings. We decompose
\bar{G}
into
t
perfect matchings and color edges in each perfect matching with a distinct color.
Overall we have used
t
colors. Since
|{\cal{M}}|=t
, we can map the
i
-th color to integer
i\in{\cal{M}}
.

Recall that
\psi
is a one-to-one mapping between
\bar{E}
and
E
, hence via
\psi
we also obtain a coloring for
E
(by coloring each edge in
E
with the same color as its corresponding edge in
\bar{E}
). Recall that
G
is 1-regular. Thus we can extend the edge coloring to a vertex coloring, such that each vertex in
G
is colored with the same color as the unique edge incident to it.

Define functions
f
and
\hat{f}
.

Consider every vertex set
\bar{U}_{k}
. We know
\bar{U}_{k}
contains
t
vertices, and let
\bar{U}_{k}=\{u_{k_{1}},u_{k_{2}},\cdots,u_{k_{t}}\}
. By definition
u_{k_{i}}[-h]
’s are identical and
u_{k_{i}}[h]
’s are exactly the
t
elements in
{\cal{M}}
. Recall that we decompose
\bar{G}
into
t
perfect matchings and each perfect matching is colored with a unique color, we know the
t
parallel edges incident to the mega-vertex
\bar{u}_{k}
are colored with
t
distinct colors. Consequently, each vertex
u_{k_{i}}
is also colored with a distinct color. Recall the one-to-one correspondence between a vertex
u_{j}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{j}\in{\cal{M}}
. Now we define a function
f
such that
f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{i}})[-h]={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{i}}[-h]
, and
f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{i}})[h]
equals the color of
u_{k_{i}}
, where we interpret each color as a number in
\{1,\cdots,t\}
. Consequently,
(f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{1}}),f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{2}}),\cdots,f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{t}}))
is a permutation of
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{1}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{2}},\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{t}})
. Hence,
f\in Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
.

Similarly, we consider each
\bar{W}_{k}=\{w_{k_{1}},w_{k_{2}},\cdots,w_{k_{t}}\}
and define a function
\hat{f}
such that
\hat{f}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{i}})[-h]={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{i}}[-h]
, and
\hat{f}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{i}})[h]
equals the color of
w_{k_{i}}
. Consequently,
(\hat{f}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{1}}),\hat{f}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{2}}),\cdots,\hat{f}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{t}}))
is also a permutation of
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{1}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{2}},\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}_{k_{t}})
, and
\hat{f}\in Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
.

Furthermore, the color of each
u_{i}
or
w_{j}
is defined as the color of the edge incident to it, hence if there is an edge between
u_{i}
and
w_{j}
in
G
, then we know
(f(u_{i}))[h]=(\hat{f}(w_{j}))[h]
. Hence, Lemma
21
is proved.
∎

See Figure
2
for an illustration of the mapping
f,\hat{f}
we construct in Lemma
21
. Iteratively applying Lemma
21
, we are able to prove the following.

Proof.

We prove the following statement by induction: For
0\leq k\leq\upsilon-1
, there exist
h
-shufflers
f_{h},\hat{f}_{h}\in Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
for every
0\leq h\leq k
such that
F_{k}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}})[h^{\prime}]=\hat{F}_{k}(\pi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}))[h^{\prime}]
for any
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}\in{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}
and
h^{\prime}\leq k
, where
F_{k}=f_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}
and
\hat{F}_{k}=\hat{f}_{k}\circ\hat{f}_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{f}_{0}
.

The statement is true for
k=1
by Lemma
21
. Suppose the statement is true for
k
, we prove it is true for
k+1
.

Consider
\hat{f}_{k}\circ\hat{f}_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{f}_{0}\circ\pi\circ f_{0}^{-1}\circ f_{1}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{k}^{-1}
. According to Lemma
21
, there exist
(k+1)
-shufflers
f_{k+1},\hat{f}_{k+1}
such that

\displaystyle\left(f_{k+1}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}})\right)[k+1]=\left(\left(\hat{f}_{k+1}\circ\hat{f}_{k}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{f}_{0}\circ\pi\circ f_{0}^{-1}\circ f_{2}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{k}^{-1}\right)({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}})\right)[k+1],\quad\forall{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}\in{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}.

(7)

Since
f_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1}\in Aut({\cal{M}}^{\upsilon})
, for every
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}\in{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}
there exists some
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}\in{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}
such that
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf y$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf y$}}}=\left(f_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1}\right)({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}})
, plug this into Equation (
7
), for all
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}}\in{\cal{M}}^{\upsilon}
we get

\displaystyle\left(f_{k+1}\circ f_{k}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}\right)({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}})[k+1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\left(\hat{f}_{k+1}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{f}_{0}\circ\pi\circ f_{0}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{k}^{-1}\circ f_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}\right)({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}})\right)[k+1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\left(\hat{f}_{k+1}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{f}_{0}\circ\pi\right)({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}})\right)[k+1]

Moreover, for any
h\leq k
, recall that
f_{k+1}
and
\hat{f}_{k+1}
does not change the
h
-th coordinate, hence

\displaystyle\left(f_{k+1}\circ f_{k}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}\right)({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}})[h]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(f_{k}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}\right)({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}})[h]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\left(\hat{f}_{k}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{f}_{1}\circ\pi\right)({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}})\right)[h]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\left(\hat{f}_{k+1}\circ\hat{f}_{k}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{f}_{0}\circ\pi\right)({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf z$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf z$}}})\right)[h]

Hence, the statement holds for all
k\leq\upsilon-1
, and Lemma
8
is proved.
∎

Appendix F
Construction of the Scheduling Instance

Now we provide the details of the reduction. We first recall all the functions and parameters we have set in proving Lemma
7
.

•

Recall that
\tau
is the one-to-one mapping that maps
i
to
k
for every
(z_{i}\oplus\neg z_{k})\in C_{2}
.

•

Apply Lemma
6
by taking
N=n
, we get
\sigma:\mathbb{Z}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{n^{\prime}}
where
n^{\prime}=n^{1+{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log\log n}})}
,
\sigma(i)=\sum_{j=0}^{\gamma}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[j]x^{j}
for
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[j]\in{\cal{S}}_{d}
where
\gamma=\lceil\frac{\log n}{\log\log n}\rceil+O(\frac{\log n}{(\log\log n)^{3/2}})
,
d=e^{{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\log\log n})}\log n
and
x=5d+1
. We lift the dimension such that
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}=({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[0],\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[\gamma+3])
where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[\gamma+1]={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[\gamma+2]={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i}[\gamma+3]=0
.

•

Apply Lemma
6
again by setting
N=4\gamma+4
, we get another injection
\sigma^{\prime}
such that
\sigma^{\prime}(y)=o(\log^{2}n)<x^{2}
for
y\leq 4\gamma+4
.

•

We have constructed in the proof of Lemma
7
:
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{0}_{i}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{i},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{1}_{i},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{2}_{i},\cdots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{2\gamma+2}_{i}=\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{2\gamma+2}_{k}
,
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{2\gamma+1}_{k}
,
\cdots
,
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{1}_{k},\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{0}_{k}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf a$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf a$}}}_{k}
where
k=\tau(i)
.

•

Again, each vector
\textstyle\bf c
represents the polynomial
\sum_{i}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}[i]x^{i}
. Polynomials and vectors are used interchangeably.

•

Let
\sigma_{max}=x^{\gamma+6}=n^{1+O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log\log n}})}
, and thus
\sigma_{max}>x\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}_{i}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}
and
\sigma_{max}>x\cdot\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{h}_{i}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}
for any
i,h
, and also
\sigma_{max}>\sigma^{\prime}(y)x^{\gamma+3}
for any
y\leq 4\gamma+4
.

Construction of the scheduling instance.

We shall construct two major classes of jobs, gap jobs and main jobs. Main jobs are divided into
5
types: dummy jobs, clause jobs, truth-assignment jobs, link jobs and variable jobs.
The three types – truth-assignment, link and variable jobs – are further divided into sub-types, e.g., variable jobs are further divided into 4 sub-types (see Table
1
). A gap job is defined as a fixed huge value
10^{14}\sigma_{max}
subtracting several main jobs.

The processing time of each job can be expressed as a summation over three components: Type, Index and True/False. The type component of a main job is always of the form
10^{j}\sigma_{max}
where
2\leq j\leq 13
. Table
1
summarizes the value
j
for each kind of main job, e.g., the type-component of a variable job whose sub-type belongs to V
⋅,+,1
is
10^{5}\sigma_{max}
. The index-component of clause jobs, truth-assignment jobs and variable jobs is of the form
10\sigma(i)
for some index
i
. Dummy jobs do not have index-component; Link jobs have much more complicated index-components, which will be specified in the following part of this subsection. Each main job has a true version and a false version. A gap job does not have a true/false version but only one unified version.

⋅,a

⋅,b

⋅,d

⋅,-

Type-component of main jobs

Define a function
\zeta
that maps the (sub)-type of a main job to the exponent of
10
as indicated by Table
1
, e.g.,
\zeta(\textrm{TR}_{\cdot,a})=11
.
Now we provide the exact processing time of every job. In the following
\rho\in\{T,F\}
,
\iota\in\{+,-\}
.

\bullet
Variable jobs: 4 jobs
V_{i,+,1}^{\rho}
and
V_{i,+,2}^{\rho}
are
constructed for the positive literal
z_{i}
, and 4 jobs
V_{i,-,1}^{\rho}
and
V_{i,-,2}^{\rho}
are
for the negative literal
\neg z_{i}
.

\displaystyle s(V_{i,\iota,\kappa}^{T})=10^{\zeta(\textrm{V}_{\cdot,\iota,k})}\sigma_{max}+10\sigma(i)+1,

\displaystyle s(V_{i,\iota,\kappa}^{F})=10^{\zeta(\textrm{V}_{\cdot,\iota,k})}\sigma_{max}+10\sigma(i)+2,\quad\kappa=1,2,\iota=+,-

\bullet
Truth-assignment jobs: 8 jobs
\textrm{TR}_{i,a}^{\rho}
,
\textrm{TR}_{i,b}^{\rho}
,

\textrm{TR}_{i,c}^{\rho}
and
\textrm{TR}_{i,d}^{\rho}
are constructed for every
i
.

\displaystyle s(\textrm{TR}_{i,\kappa}^{T})=10^{\zeta(\textrm{TR}_{\cdot,\kappa})}\sigma_{max}+10\sigma(i)+1.5,

\displaystyle s(\textrm{TR}_{i,\kappa}^{F})=10^{\zeta(\textrm{TR}_{\cdot,\kappa})}\sigma_{max}+10\sigma(i)+1.\quad\kappa=a,b,c,d

\bullet
Clause jobs: there are 3 clause jobs for every clause
cl_{\ell}\in C_{1}

where
\ell\in\{2,5,\cdots,n-1\}
, with one
\textrm{CL}_{\ell}^{T}
and two copies of
\textrm{CL}_{\ell}^{F}
:

s(\textrm{CL}_{\ell}^{T})=10^{\zeta(\textrm{CL}_{\cdot})}\sigma_{max}+10\sigma(\ell)+2,\quad s(\textrm{CL}_{\ell}^{F})=10^{\zeta(\textrm{CL}_{\cdot})}\sigma_{max}+10\sigma(\ell)+1.

\bullet
Dummy jobs: there are
n+n/3
true dummy jobs
\textrm{DM}^{T}
of processing time
10^{\zeta(\textrm{DM})}\sigma_{max}+1
, and
n-n/3

false dummy jobs
\textrm{DM}^{F}
of processing time
10^{\zeta(\textrm{DM})}\sigma_{max}+2
.

\bullet
Link jobs: We create
4\gamma+4
links jobs for each clause in
C_{2}
.
Recall the vectors
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}_{i}
and
\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{h}_{i}
for
1\leq h\leq 2\gamma+2
. For every clause
(z_{i}\oplus z_{k})\in C_{2}
and every
1\leq h\leq 2\gamma+2
, we create two pairs of link jobs, LN
{}_{i,h,+}^{T}
and LN
{}_{i,h,+}^{F}
, and LN
{}_{k,h,-}^{T}
and LN
{}_{k,h,-}^{F}
such that

s(\textrm{LN}_{i,h,+}^{T})=10^{\zeta(\textrm{LN}_{\cdot,+})}\sigma_{max}+10{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}(i){\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}+1,\quad s(\textrm{LN}_{i,h,+}^{F})=10^{\zeta(\textrm{LN}_{\cdot,+})}\sigma_{max}+10{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}_{i}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}+2,

s(\textrm{LN}_{k,h,-}^{T})=10^{\zeta(\textrm{LN}_{\cdot,-})}\sigma_{max}+10\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{h}(k){\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}+1,\quad s(\textrm{LN}_{k,h,-}^{F})=10^{\zeta(\textrm{LN}_{\cdot,-})}\sigma_{max}+10\hat{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}}^{h}(i){\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}+2.

Let

\textrm{TR}_{A}
,
\textrm{TR}_{B}
,
\textrm{TR}_{C}
,
\textrm{TR}_{D}
be the set of jobs
\textrm{TR}_{i,a}^{\rho}
,
\textrm{TR}_{i,b}^{\rho}
,

\textrm{TR}_{i,c}^{\rho}
and
\textrm{TR}_{i,d}^{\rho}
respectively. Sometimes we may
drop the superscript for simplicity, e.g., we use
\textrm{TR}_{i,a}
to represent

\textrm{TR}_{i,a}^{T}
or
\textrm{TR}_{i,a}^{F}
. We construct gap jobs. There are 5 kinds of gap jobs.

for each variable

\displaystyle s(\theta_{\textrm{V-L},i,+})

\displaystyle=

Appendix G
Proof of Theorem
2

The proof is carried out in 4 steps. We first show in Section
G.1
that every job in the constructed instance has a unique processing time. This allows us to refer to a job by its symbol (e.g.,
V_{i,+,1}^{T}
) as well as by its processing time. Next, we show in Section
G.2
that if a significant fraction of clauses in the 3SAT
′
instance are satisfiable, then the constructed scheduling instance admits a solution with a small objective value. Next, we show in Section
G.3
that if any truth-assignment for the 3SAT
′
instance will leave a significant fraction of clauses unsatisfied, then the constructed scheduling instance does not admit a solution with a small objective value. Finally, we are able to prove the correctness of our reduction in Section
G.4
by leveraging the above two facts.

G.1
Uniqueness of job processing times

We claim that the processing time of each job we create is unique, whereas there is a one-to-one correspondence between the symbol of a job and its processing time. To see the claim, consider Table
2
. It suffices to compare the processing time of jobs within each subtype. Given that
\sigma
is an injection, it is easy to see that the processing time of each variable job, truth-assignment job, clause job, variable-dummy job and variable-truth job is unique. For variable-clause-dummy jobs, by property 4 of Lemma
6
we know the sum
\sigma(\ell)+\sigma(i)
for
i=\ell-1,\ell,\ell+1
is unique. The uniqueness of link jobs follows from the uniqueness of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}_{i}
’s from Lemma
9
. The uniqueness of link-link jobs follows from the uniqueness of the summation
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h}_{i}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{h+1}_{i}
from Lemma
10
.

G.2
3SAT
′
to Scheduling

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 22
.

If there are at most
\vartheta n
clauses which are not satisfied, then the constructed scheduling instance admits a feasible schedule with objective value at most
(10^{14}\sigma_{max})^{q}(2\gamma n+8n)+\vartheta n\cdot\frac{q(q-1)}{2}(10^{14}\sigma_{max})^{q-2}+o(n\sigma_{max}^{q-2})
.

Recall that every main job, except the clause job, admits a true copy and false copy, while the clause job admits a true copy and two false copies. We first ignore the true/false version of jobs and schedule them according to Table
3
, where each row represents jobs that are scheduled on one machine.

SAT to Scheduling – Jobs scheduled on each machine

We show that if we schedule according to Table
3
, then every job has been scheduled (ignoring the superscripts
T
or
F
, which will be determined later). It is obvious that every gap job is scheduled. For simplicity, we abuse the notation a bit by using the symbol of a gap job to denote the machine on which it is scheduled.

\bullet
Consider clause jobs. Recall that for each clause
cl_{\ell}
and
i=\ell-1,\ell,\ell+1
, we either construct

\theta_{\textrm{V-D},i,-}
and
\theta_{\textrm{V-C-D},\ell,i,+}
if the positive literal
z_{i}
occurs in
C_{1}
, or
construct
\theta_{\textrm{V-D},i,+}
and
\theta_{\textrm{V-C-D},\ell,i,-}
if the negative literal
\neg z_{i}
occurs in
C_{1}
. Hence the three copies of job
\textrm{CL}_{\ell}
appear on machine
\theta_{\textrm{V-C-D},\ell,\ell-1,+}
or
\theta_{\textrm{V-C-D},\ell,\ell-1,-}
, machine
\theta_{\textrm{V-C-D},\ell,\ell,+}
or
\theta_{\textrm{V-C-D},\ell,\ell,-}
, and machine
\theta_{\textrm{V-C-D},\ell,\ell+1,+}
or
\theta_{\textrm{V-C-D},\ell,\ell+1,-}
. Thus, all three copies of a clause job are scheduled.

\bullet
Consider truth-assignment jobs. There are two copies of TR
i,a
, TR
i,b
, TR
i,c
and TR
i,d
. It is easy to see that all of them are scheduled on machines
\theta_{\textrm{V-T},i,a,c}
,
\theta_{\textrm{V-T},i,b,d}
,
\theta_{\textrm{V-T},i,a,d}
and
\theta_{\textrm{V-T},i,b,c}
.

\bullet
Consider variable jobs. There are two copies of
V_{i,+,1}
,
V_{i,+,2}
,
V_{i,-,1}
and
V_{i,-,2}
. It is easy to see that one copy of them are scheduled on machines
\theta_{\textrm{V-T},i,a,c}
,
\theta_{\textrm{V-T},i,b,d}
,
\theta_{\textrm{V-T},i,a,d}
and
\theta_{\textrm{V-T},i,b,c}
. One copy of
V_{i,+,2}
and
V_{i,-,2}
are scheduled on machines
\theta_{\textrm{V-L},i,+}
and
\theta_{\textrm{V-L},i,-}
. If machines

\theta_{\textrm{V-D},i,-}
and
\theta_{\textrm{V-C-D},\ell,i,+}
exist (when the positive literal
z_{i}
occurs in
C_{1}
), then
V_{i,-,1}
and
V_{i,+,1}
are scheduled on them respectively; otherwise machines
\theta_{\textrm{V-D},i,+}
and
\theta_{\textrm{V-C-D},\ell,i,-}
exist (the negative literal
\neg z_{i}
occurs in
C_{1}
), then
V_{i,+,1}
and
V_{i,-,1}
are scheduled on them respectively.

\bullet
Consider link jobs. There are two copies of LN
i,h,+
(or LN
i,h,-
) for
1\leq h\leq 2\gamma+2
. Let
\iota\in\{+,-\}
. The two copies of LN
i,1,ι
are scheduled on machines
\theta_{\textrm{V-L},i,\iota}
and
\theta_{\textrm{L-L},i,1,\iota}
. The two copies of LN
i,h,ι
are scheduled on
\theta_{\textrm{L-L},i,h,\iota}
and
\theta_{\textrm{L-L},i,h+1,\iota}
for
2\leq h\leq 2\gamma+1
. The two copies of LN
i,2γ+2,+
are scheduled on machines
\theta_{\textrm{L-L},2\gamma+1,+}
and
\theta_{\textrm{L-L},i,+,-}
, and the two copies of LN
i,2γ+2,-
are scheduled on machines
\theta_{\textrm{L-L},2\gamma+1,-}
and
\theta_{\textrm{L-L},\tau^{-1}(i),+,-}
, where
\tau^{-1}
is the inverse of the mapping
\tau
(note that
\tau^{-1}
exists since
\tau
is one-to-one).

\bullet
Consider dummy jobs. There are in total
2n
dummy jobs. It is obvious that for every
i
, 2 dummy jobs are scheduled on machines
\theta_{\textrm{V-C-D},\ell,i,+}
,
\theta_{\textrm{V-D},i,-}
or machines
\theta_{\textrm{V-C-D},\ell,i,-}
,
\theta_{\textrm{V-D},i,+}
.

Next, we consider the load of every machine. According to Table
2
, it is easy to verify that if we sum up the type-component of jobs on each machine, it becomes
10^{14}\sigma_{max}
; if we sum up the index-component of jobs on each machine, it becomes
0
. Now we consider the T/F-component of jobs. It is easy to verify that the T/F-components of all jobs add up to
0
, hence we have the following direct observation.

Observation 5
.

The total processing time of all jobs add up to
10^{14}\sigma_{max}\cdot(2\gamma n+8n)
.
